Oman bunker demand stable in Oct Oman's bunker market remained broadly stable in October,
with demand and supply levels largely unchanged from September. However, increased vessel traffic and port congestion at key ports, particularly Duqm and Sohar, resulted in operational delays and longer vessel waiting times, two Oman-based market participants, including traders and suppliers, told Platts Nov. 4.
"Demand and supply remained generally stable in October, with volumes at similar levels to the previous month. The main challenge was port congestion, particularly at Duqm and Sohar, where increased vessel traffic caused operational delays. Some vessels have remained in Oman amid restrictions on transiting the Strait of Hormuz, contributing to increased traffic at the country's ports. Other challenges included longer vessel waiting times, berth availability and scheduling constraints. Earliest bunker delivery dates were about two to three days," an Omanbased supplier told Platts.
"Marine gasoil sales were about 4,900 mt in October, compared with 4,981 mt in September," the supplier said.
At the Strait of Hormuz, vessel traffic dropped to19 transits from 21 on Nov. 1, around 24% below October's daily average of 25. The Nov. 2 total comprised 10 vessels entering the Persian Gulf and nine exiting, CAS analysts said in the report.
"Bunker sales at Salalah declined in October amid lower inquiry volumes and price-sensitive buying, despite an increase in vessel arrivals. Even when buyers initially inquire for 50 mt, they sometimes reduce their requirements to 20-25 mt after hearing the prices. October volumes were around 2,000 mt, down about 500 mt from the previous month," according to a Salalah-based supplier Nov. 4.

Sri Lanka bunker sales rise on stronger fuel oil demand in October
Strong HSFO uptake drives Oct bunker activity
Sri Lanka bunker calls climb despite OPL weather disruptions
Sri Lanka's October bunker sales rose from September as stronger high sulfur fuel oil demand, improving weather and ample product availability supported activity across key ports, market sources said.
"October bunker volumes increased compared with the previous month, with total sales across suppliers estimated at about 95,000-100,000 mt, compared with about 95,000 mt in September," a Sri Lanka-based supplier told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Nov. 5. "Weather conditions have also started improving."
Demand for HSFO was particularly strong during October, with average stem sizes increasing to about 2,000-2,500 metric tons from the previous range of 800-1,400 mt, the supplier said. HSFO sales also exceeded those of very low sulfur fuel oil in October, which is unusual as VLSFO is typically the main grade supplied, the supplier said. "The stronger HSFO demand was partly due to reports of limited HSFO availability in the market,", the supplier said.
Total bunkering and ship-to-ship calls at Colombo, Trincomalee and Hambantota increased to 302 in October from 233 in September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
"Bunker availability remains good, with suppliers continuing to import cargoes consistently," the supplier said. "This has resulted in relatively ample supply, with import activity putting pressure

Bunker inquiries in Colombo and Hambantota were pricesensitive in October, while suppliers maintained healthy volumes despite weather-related disruption.
"In October, the overall market fluctuated: when prices were down, inquiries were up; when prices were up, inquiries were down," a Colombo-based trader said. "Overall availability for all three grades was good throughout the month."
Platts assessed marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Colombo at $950/mt on Nov. 4, down $35/mt month over month. Marine fuel oil 0.5% delivered to Trincomalee was assessed at $1,025/mt, up $15/mt.
"Colombo and Hambantota OPL supplies were disrupted due to weather; however, volumes were favorable despite the bunker supply postponements," a trader said. "We supplied nearly 6,0007,000 mt of VLSFO in Colombo and 10,000 mt in Trincomalee."
"Demand for marine gas oil was consistent throughout the month compared with previous months," the trader said.
Platts assessed Bunker FO 380 CST delivered to Colombo at $848/mt on Nov. 4, up $22/mt month over month.

East coast bunker demand mixed in October The east coast bunker market faced product availability
issues in October, with steady inquiries at Paradip contrasting with supply constraints at Haldia, Visakhapatnam, Chennai and Tuticorin, according to market sources.
"We have seen a good number of inquiries in October, with an average demand overall with a volume of 10,000-11,000 mt," a Paradip-based supplier said. "We have noticed that vessels took small quantities of parcels, with an average stem size of 400500 mt, possibly due to high pricing. On the availability side, the product was good, and it was imported from Kandla and Haldia, with existing inventories in Paradip."
"Demand for HSFO was decent, with an average stem size ranging from 3,00 mt to 4,00 mt. Due to barge availability issues, we have supplied only 16,00 mt by Tank trucks, but in December and January we get additional barges to supply more HSFO, even at anchorage," the supplier added.
"At the port of Haldia, the overall port volume was 8,0009,000 mt. Demand was also strong on the availability side in the first week of October; there was no product availabilities and there were barge issues at the port," a Visakhapatnam-based trader said.
"In Chennai, no product is available for October due to issues at the refinery, so there is no volume for the month. Even though we have inquiries, we have diverted them to other ports", the trader added.
"However, in Tuticorin, supply was tight, with almost no product available for nearly 20 days in the month, even though they were able to do 8,000 mt of volume for the month, and in Tuticorin port also there is no demand seen overall; only 4,000 mt of volume seen in the port", according to the trader.
"At Kakinada port, supply and demand were good throughout the month, with a good number of inquiries and no drawbacks; however, in Visakhapatnam, availability was tight for both HSFO and VLSFO throughout the month, and demand was moderate compared to the previous month," the second Dubai-based trader operating in the Indian markets said.
-- Sai deepak Tarlada, Abhishek Anupam, Ashutosh Dubey

Resid
Hong Kong ex-wharf term HSFO contracts for November rise 22% MOM: traders
Contract premiums rise $25-$30/mt: traders
Hi-5 spread widens on weaker HSFO demand
Term contract premiums for November-loading ex-wharf 380CST fuel oil cargoes in the North Asian bunkering hub of Hong Kong maintained their upward trend for the second month in a row, rising 21.78% month over month, according to Hong Kongbased bunker traders.
Term ex-wharf high sulfur fuel oil contracts for November were concluded at premiums of $150-$152/metric ton over the benchmark FOB Singapore 380CST fuel oil cargoes, up $25-$30/ mt from November levels, the traders told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Similar to September, tight HSFO cargo availabilities and higher freight rates were attributed to the higher ex-wharf term deal levels. The premium levels for November are the highest in 2026, with traders expecting price increases in December if more stock is not made available.
"HSFO really has a high premium this month, mainly due to all the supply shortages," said a Hong Kong-based bunker trader, adding that "while there might be some decline as crude prices are falling, recent fluctuations have been too volatile."
Another Hong Kong-based bunker trader noted that while ex-wharf purchases were expensive, "delivered prices are still maintaining at relatively high levels as compared to before," keeping ex-wharf deals commercially viable despite the elevated premiums.
Platts assessed the premium for Hong Kong-delivered 380CST fuel oil over the FOB Singapore cargo value at an average of $159.35/mt for October. The premium has since risen to a month-to-date average of $160.07/mt for November and was last assessed at $165.79/mt on Nov. 4.
At the same time, term ex-wharf marine fuel 0.5%S contracts for November also reached premiums of $135-$140/mt over the benchmark FOB Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S cargo, according

to Hong Kong-based bunker traders. This marked an increase of $13-$20/mt from October.
Platts assessed the premium for Hong Kong-delivered marine fuel 0.5%S over the benchmark FOB Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S cargoes at an average of $95.83/mt in October. The premium has since risen to a month-to-date average of $99.62/mt in November and was last assessed at $103.76/mt Nov. 4.
The spread between the delivered Hong Kong marine fuel 0.5%S and 380CST HSFO, or the Hi-5 spread, narrowed to its lowest at $1/mt Oct. 14. The narrowed spread was primarily due to tightened supply of HSFO in Zhoushan and South China ports and operational disruptions due to Typhoon Champi, raising premiums for prompt loaders.
The Hi-5 spread has since widened on the back of lower demand for HSFO to a month-to-date average of $28/mt in November, up $8.86/mt from October's average, according to traders. The spread last stood at $26/mt on Nov. 4.
Singapore's commercial stockpiles of heavy distillates dipped 0.9% week over week to a two-week low of 18.68 million barrels in the week ended Oct. 28, according to Enterprise Singapore data released late Oct. 29, partly due to a rise in exports. Stockpiles were at their lowest since Oct. 14.
Term ex-wharf low sulfur marine gasoil contracts for November cargoes rose to premiums of about $26/mt over the benchmark FOB Singapore low sulfur marine gasoil with 0.1% sulfur cargo, according to Hong Kong-based bunker traders. The premium range increased $11-$11.10/mt from October.
Platts assessed the premium for Hong Kong-delivered low sulfur marine gasoil with 0.1% sulfur over the FOB Singapore low sulfur marine gasoil with 0.1% sulfur cargo value at an average of $18.05/mt in October. The premium has since risen to a monthto-date average of $51.82/mt in November and was last assessed at $52.57/mt on Nov. 4.
In the short term, traders noted some tightness in the marine gasoil complex, although replenishment cargo arrivals next week are expected to ease inventories in H2 November.
-- Kahlil Sudhakaran

Platts European Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
Loading delays tighten ARA fuel supplies
VLSFO exports continue despite tight market
Ongoing loading delays affected both very low sulfur fuel oil and high sulfur fuel oil in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp region Nov. 5, while arbitrage inflow expectations from Latin America were lower.
European VLSFO markets remain tight within the ARA region amid ongoing supply tightness and stable demand.
Market participants note that this tightness stems from loading delays within ARA terminals.
In the Mediterranean, tightness persists, with participants noting steady local refinery production across both the West and East Mediterranean.
However, VLSFO product continues to be fixed out of Europe to the Americas and Singapore, supported by falling freight rates.
The LSFO market remains stable across ARA and Mediterranean hubs, despite higher natural gas and LNG prices, which compete with 1% fuel oil product for power generation.
"LSFO is balanced, I think," a Northwest Europe-based trader said.
In the high sulfur fuel oil market, availability remained tight across both Northwest Europe and the Mediterranean, with participants citing reduced inflows from Latin America and ongoing blending constraints.
In addition to the reduced flows, the cost and availability of cutter stocks continued to restrict production of finished RMGgrade material.
"There's oil on offer for sure; the issue is RMG finished grade," a trader said.
Similar fundamentals were seen in Northwest Europe, with one bunker trader noting a two- to three-day delay at a terminal.
There were no trades in the Platts Market on Close assessment process for fuel oil cargoes.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Tommy Petrou

Feedstocks
Naphtha East-West spread hits one-month low on weaker freight rates
Shipping rates fall, reducing Asia landed prices
US midterm election result eases Iran war fears
The naphtha East-West spread fell to a one-month low Nov. 4, pressured by a sharp correction in freight rates, reducing the cost of moving barrels into Asia.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month East-West spread -- the spread between the CFR Japan naphtha cargo swap and the CIF NWE equivalent -- at $64/metric ton on Nov. 4, the narrowest since Sept. 29, when it stood at $61.50/mt.
Market participants attributed much of the weakness in the east-west spread to falling clean tanker freight rates, which have weighed on the delivered cost of naphtha in Asia.
"It's mainly due to weaker freight markets adding pressure to the naphtha east-west market," a participant active in the European naphtha paper market said.
The decline in freight has fed directly into naphtha east-west economics. With the cost of shipping naphtha from the Middle East to Asia falling, Asian buyers do not need to pay as large a premium over European naphtha values to attract barrels eastward.
The lower freight requirement therefore reduces the value needed for the East-West spread to clear the arbitrage, putting downward pressure on the spread.
Market participants also indirectly linked the correction in tanker freight to the US midterm elections, through expectations regarding US energy and sanctions policy.
The results of the US midterm election eased fears of an escalation in the US-Iran conflict and weighed on prices across the energy complex. The Democratic win in the midterm elections means the party will have an increased ability to scrutinize the war in Iran and greater control over restrictions on funding it.
"I think the midterms played a role in TC5 pricing off directly and indirectly on East-West, as freight rates came off," the participant added.
Platts assessed the clean Arab Gulf to Japan 55,000 mt LongRange 1 tanker freight at $219.88/mt on Nov. 4, down from a high of $237.65/mt on Oct. 27.
-- Barbara Fernandez-pita
Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Naphtha Daily Market Analysis
Asian buyers still cautious
South Korea's GS Caltex buys naphtha for H2 Dec delivery
Open interest for Nov MOPAG naphtha swaps more than doubles

The Asian naphtha complex retreated from recent highs Nov. 5, as front-month Brent crude futures plunged below the $100/b mark following the Democratic Party's win in the US midterm elections.
Although the softer valuations suggest that concerns over further escalation of the US-Iran conflict have eased, some market participants remain cautious and are biding their time before taking positions.
"With where crude is, I think market is pricing a de-escalation," a Singapore-based trader said.
Nevertheless, several market sources highlighted the absence of spot buying interest among Asian end users.
"Since no end users [are] buying spot CFR [naphtha cargoes] today [Nov. 5], the market can be quiet again," a source at a regional trading house said. "People are waiting [to see] what will happen next...nobody knows."
Platts assessed the CFR Japan cash differential against the benchmark Mean of Platts Japan naphtha at $57/metric ton at the Asian close Nov. 4, down from $58/mt a day earlier, while the CFR Japan naphtha physical crack against front-month ICE Brent crude futures stood at $197.525/mt, narrowing from $205.6/mt a day ago.
Brokers pegged the balance-month November-December Mean of Platts Japan swap time spread at $33/mt in midmorning Asian trading Nov. 5, up from $30/mt at the Asian close Nov. 4.
In tenders, South Korea's GS Caltex bought grade B and C heavy full-range naphtha cargoes at a premium of $50s-$60s/ mt to MOPJ naphtha assessments on a CFR basis for delivery in H2 December, while Skikda-origin naphtha cargoes were at a premium of $70s/mt to MOPJ naphtha assessments, Platts reported Nov. 4.
Open interest for front-month November MOPAG naphtha swaps more than doubled, rising 114.71% month over month to 73,000 mt, the latest Intercontinental Exchange data showed.
The Platts front-month FOB Arab Gulf naphtha swap averaged $732.89/mt in October, inching up 3.81% from September's $705.98/mt and $673.74/mt in August.
The FOB Fujairah naphtha cargo price averaged $805.37/mt in May, falling 6.23% from the previous month's average, Platts data showed.
Taiwan's naphtha consumption rebounded 24.2% month over month but fell 12.8% year over year to 1.073 million mt in September, as several naphtha-fed steam crackers operated at higher run rates.
Olefin margins rose slightly above the typical breakeven levels of $250/mt for integrated producers. The CFR Northeast Asia ethylene-CFR Japan naphtha physical spread -- closely watched by petrochemical producers -- averaged $276.84/ mt in September, up from $227.29/mt. The breakeven level for non-integrated producers stands at $300-$350/mt, Platts data showed.

Platts European Naphtha Daily Market Analysis
Trading stays subdued amid thin supply Freight rate drops pressure East-West spread

The front-month East-West spread -- the spread between the CFR Japan naphtha cargo swap and the CIF NWE equivalent -- was at $63.50/mt, down 50 cents/mt day over day.
-- Barbara Fernandez-pita

European physical naphtha trading remained subdued Nov. 5, with thin supply met by equally limited demand across grades.
Gasoline blending appetite has softened as margins retreat from earlier highs, while petrochemical demand also remains lackluster.
Lower outright naphtha prices could offer some relief to cracker economics, with naphtha retaining a feedstock advantage over propane and butane, particularly in Asia.
The naphtha East-West spread has been falling, pressured by a sharp correction in freight rates and an unwinding in the Iran war premiums.
"Had real guys offering the East-West down at the start of the day," a player active in the naphtha paper market said.
Market participants attributed much of the weakness in the east-west spread to falling clean tanker freight rates, which have weighed on the delivered cost of naphtha in Asia.
"It's mainly due to weaker freight markets adding pressure to the naphtha East-West market," a second participant active in the European naphtha paper market said.
The decline in freight has fed directly into the East-West economics of naphtha. With the cost of shipping naphtha from the Middle East to Asia falling, Asian buyers do not need to pay as large a premium over European naphtha values to attract barrels eastward.
The lower freight requirement therefore reduces the value needed for the East-West spread to clear the arbitrage, putting downward pressure on the spread.
Market participants also indirectly linked the correction in tanker freight to the US midterm elections, through expectations regarding US energy and sanctions policy.
The Democratic Party sweep in the US midterm election eased fears of an escalation in the war with Iran and weighed on prices across the energy complex. The results so far mean that the winning party will have an increased ability to scrutinize the conflict and greater control over restrictions on funding it.
"I think the midterms played a role in TC5 pricing off directly and indirectly on East-West, as freight rates came off," the second participant added.
"De-escalation is the main reason," the first player active in the naphtha paper market said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price at $851.25/metric ton, down $19/mt day over day.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha swap down $17.50/mt at $798.00/mt, while the crack was up 35 cents/barrel at $4.00/b.

Asia, Middle East propane swap trades on CME fall 2.75% MOM in October

The total volume of traded propane swaps settled on the Chicago Mercantile Exchange fell 2.75% month over month to 3.111 million metric tons in October, according to the latest data on CME's website, as seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Nov. 4.
Leading the decline, volumes for propane FEI futures traded on the CME fell 6.44% month over month to 2.555 million mt in October, while trading volumes for propane Saudi CP futures jumped 18.80% over the same period to 556,000 mt, the data showed.
Propane prices rose over the same period, with Platts assessing the front-month CFR North Asia propane swap at an average of $943.91/mt in October, up 13.51% from the September average of $831.55/mt.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB Saudi CP swaps at an average of $707.45/mt in October, up 8.69% from an average of $650.91/mt in September.
LPG markets in Asia and the Middle East were dominated by tight supply in October, as maritime disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait, related to the Middle East conflict, restricted cargo flows from the region.
The butane premium over propane in Asia and the Middle East widened to more than four-month highs at the Oct. 19 market close, as strong seasonal demand for winter gasoline blending and supply disruptions in the Middle East tightened butane availability. The butane market had remained tight for the past two to three weeks, with availability showing limited signs of easing in the near term.
China, the world's largest LPG importer, took in 2.312 million metric tons in October, a decrease of 0.77% month over month, to according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data as of Nov. 4.
The US remained China's largest LPG supplier in October, shipping 1.248 million mt, up 13.04% from 1.104 million mt in September. US cargoes accounted for 54% of China's total LPG imports, compared with 47.4% a month earlier.

and Middle East propane contracts traded on the CME rose in October, up 20.79% to 860,000 mt, after slipping 0.18% month over month in September, CME data showed.

North Asia LPG prices drop for fourth straight day

CFR North Asia LPG prices fell for a fourth consecutive session at the Nov. 5 Asian close, pressured by a $67.44/metricton decline in front-month Brent futures as concerns over a potential escalation in the US-Iran conflict eased following the Democratic Party's midterm election victory.

Platts assessed CFR South China propane at $991/mt on Nov. 5, down $73/mt day over day.
Three bids were heard during the trading session Nov. 5, but no trades were concluded at the market close.

Taiwan's LPG demand declined 13.2% month over month and fell 25.4% year over year to 156,023 mt in September, data from the Ministry of Economic Affairs' Energy Administration showed Nov. 2 .
In statistical news, the total open interest for front-month November Asia and Middle East propane contracts traded on the CME rose in October, up 20.79% to 860,000 mt, after slipping 0.18% month over month in September, CME data showed .

According to CME data, open interest for front-month November FEI propane futures rose 14.67% month over month to 641,000 mt in October, while open interest for front-month November Saudi CP propane futures rose 43.14% to 219,000 mt over the same period.
In the US, propane and propylene inventories fell to their lowest since July during the week ended Oct. 30, above analyst projections as overall demand beat expectations, according to US Energy Information Administration supply and demand data published Nov. 4.
Stocks tightened by 1.627 million barrels to 104.108 million barrels, their lowest level since late July, when stocks were at 103.103 million barrels. The decline marks the fifth consecutive weekly stock draw, the first four-week span of inventory tightening since late December and into early January.
Stock levels fell below year-ago levels of 106.094 million barrels for the week ended Oct. 31, 2025, down to 104.108 million barrels, the data showed.
Total demand, including exports and domestic product supplied, rose 316,000 barrels/day to 3.309 million b/d, according to EIA data. Of that total, exports fell 364,000 b/d to 1.869 million b/d while domestic product supplied made up the difference, up 680,000 b/d to 1.44 million b/d.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Jeff Ong

Platts European Propane Daily Commentary
US inventories drop to lowest since July
CIF large cargo prices fall $15.50/mt
The Northwest European propane market faced continued competition for replacement cargoes Nov. 5, with limited spot availability and the developing heating season keeping supply security in focus.
Recent market feedback had indicated that much of the US supply available to European participants was committed under term arrangements, leaving a relatively small pool of cargoes for spot redistribution.
However, market discussions had highlighted an open arbitrage from the US, with a vessel expected to arrive in Europe in January.
Europe remained exposed to competition from Asia for Atlantic Basin shipments.
US exports had expanded to serve both regions, while Asian buyers continued to seek alternatives to constrained Middle Eastern supply, with freight costs and uncertainty surrounding shipping through the Strait of Hormuz continuing to pose logistical risks.
Within Europe, recent feedback had pointed to limited local supply, with propane retained for fuel use where economics favored consumption over sale.
Heating requirements provided an additional seasonal outlet, although the pace of demand remained dependent on temperatures, which was understood to have not had a strong uptick in demand due to mild temperature conditions.
Petrochemical consumption remained sensitive to competition from naphtha.
Flexible crackers' ability to adjust feedstock intake offered scope to moderate propane demand.
Meanwhile in the US, propane and propylene inventories fell to their lowest since July during the week ended Oct. 30,

above analyst projections as overall demand beat expectations, according to US Energy Information Administration supply and demand data published Nov. 4.
Stocks tightened by 1.627 million barrels to 104.108 million barrels, their lowest level since late July, when they were at 103.103 million barrels.

Limited supply constrains NWE butane market
US arbitrage draws cargoes away from Europe
Prices fall amid tight availability concerns
The Northwest European butane market remained constrained by limited availability Nov. 5, as winter gasoline blending requirements competed with demand from other regions for Atlantic Basin supply.
The transition to winter gasoline specifications supported butane consumption within European refineries, limiting the volume available to the merchant market.
Asian buying continued to absorb a substantial share of US shipments as importers replaced disrupted Middle Eastern supply.
Reduced butane arrivals into the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp hub during October left Europe entering November with a limited import cushion.
However "US [volumes are] coming across," a trader said on the day, highlighting potentially an attractive arbitrage opportunities from the US to Europe.
Recent market update highlighted the resulting shortage of butane supply across the region.

Platts Western Mediterranean LPG Daily Commentary
Butane supply tightens amid winter demand
West Med FOB butane drops $44 to $1,164/mt
Propane availability shrinks across region
The Mediterranean LPG market continued to face restricted supply options Nov. 5, as seasonal fuel requirements coincided with competition for cargoes from outside the region.
Butane remained particularly exposed to the tightening global balance.
Recent regional feedback had indicated limited prompt coaster availability, while winter bottling requirements across North Africa and the eastern Mediterranean provided a seasonal source of consumption.
The wider supply backdrop remained constrained. Ahead of November linked the global LPG market's firmness to seasonally higher demand and limited availability, reinforcing the challenge facing import-dependent Mediterranean buyers.
Propane availability had also tightened in recent regional discussions, leaving buyers dependent on securing replacement volumes alongside heating and autogas requirements.
Petrochemical buyers retained some flexibility to reduce LPG consumption where alternative feedstocks were more attractive.

Any broader easing in the regional balance, however, remained dependent on additional cargo arrivals and their availability for redistribution into the coaster market.
Platts assessed the West Mediterranean FOB butane market at $1,164/metric ton, down $26/mt day over day and stable as a percentage versus Platts CIF NWE naphtha at 136.75%, or at a $91/mt premium to FOB Northwest Europe coasters.
Platts assessed coaster freight from Lavera to Mohammedia at $72/mt, stable day over day, based on market information received throughout the day.
The CIF Mediterranean butane large cargo market was assessed at $911/mt, or 107% of naphtha.
Platts assessed the West Mediterranean FOB propane market at $974.50/mt, down $15.50/mt day over day.
The CIF Med propane large cargo market was assessed at $809.75/mt, or at a $17.75/mt premium to CIF Northwest Europe large cargoes.
In Morocco, Platts calculated the CIF very large cargo freight forward at $982.50/mt for delivery 45-60 days forward, down $4/mt.

Tankers
Strong crude markets continue to strain LR2 availability: d'Amico
Recovering Hormuz flows support crude carriers
D'Amico Q3 spot rates jump 37% yearly
Newbuild order book continues to expand
The strength of crude markets continued to draw in Long Range 2 tankers and shrink the available product carrier fleet, product tanker firm d'Amico International Shipping said Nov. 5.
That and the ongoing increase in product exports, especially from China "should further stimulate product freight markets in the coming months, which have again reached record levels recently," CEO Carlos Balestra di Mottola said in a statement accompanying the company's Q3 interim financial results.
This comes as additional crude exports reach refineries and as stocks of certain refined products remain critically low, Balestra di Mottola said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the global LR2 index for non-scrubber-fitted and non-eco vessels at $288,845/ day Nov. 4 and the equivalent index for Aframaxes at $181,088/day.
The company's average daily spot Time Charter Equivalent rate in Q3 rose 37% year over year to $34,940/day in Q3 and 75% for the first nine months of 2026 to $41,094/day, d'Amico said.
Clean tanker freight should remain elevated in the coming weeks, though momentum may moderate as tonnage availability improves and charterers resist high rates, analysts at S&P Global Horizons said Nov. 4. East of Suez, LR1 and LR2 markets face downward pressure from limited inquiries and lengthening position

lists, but Hormuz risks and shuttle-tanker uncertainty should preserve Persian Gulf premiums and support prompt cargoes.
West of Suez, LR freight may ease further. "MR markets remain mixed: US Gulf rates may correct as ballasters arrive, while tighter vessel availability in Asia, resilient export flows and longer voyages should support regional demand," the Horizons analysts said.
Many Chinese refineries have suspended exports of gasoil to foreign countries with immediate effect to meet domestic demand amid tight supplies, refining sources and traders told Platts Nov. 5.
Volatile geopolitics Middle Eastern oil exports have recovered substantially from

Over the last four weeks, average crude and product flows transiting Hormuz reached about three-quarters of their preconflict levels and redirected barrels -- to Yanbu, Fujairah and Ceyhan -- mean the recovery in oil exports from the Persian Gulf over this period has been closer to 90%, d'Amico said. Ship-toship transfers in the Gulf of Oman and further afield, which often involve extended waiting times, as well as vessel repositioning and the routing of certain Red Sea exports via the Cape of Good Hope, continued to reduce effective fleet availability and support freight rates, the company said.
The Persian Gulf exported 12.5 million b/d of crude in Q3, compared to 17.5 million b/d a year earlier, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. In October, it was 15.9 million b/d, the data showed.
"A recent resurgence in attacks on regional energy infrastructure and commercial vessels, nevertheless highlights the fragility of this recovery and the persistent risks affecting key maritime routes," Balestra di Mottola said.
The October diesel supply agreement between the US and Russia could facilitate a partial recovery in exports, but its impact on the global diesel market balance will likely be limited by the constrained capacity of Russia's refining system, Balestra di Mottola said. "The continuing shortfall in Russian middle-distillate exports has further tightened global product markets, increasing the need for replacement cargoes from alternative suppliers," he said.
On the supply side, the newbuilding order book has continued to expand; at the end of September 2026, it represented 16.2% of the existing Handy, Medium Range and LR1 fleet and 26.1% of the overall tanker fleet, in deadweight ton terms, d'Amico data showed. The prospective increase in vessel supply is partly mitigated by the aging profile of the fleet currently on the water, the company said.
At the end of September 2026, 22.5% of the Handy, MR and LR1 fleet and 21.3% of the overall tanker fleet, in deadweight ton terms, were 20 years old or over, while 54.7% and 47.4%, respectively, were over 15 years old, d'Amico said. Fleet aging may constrain vessel productivity and encourage higher demolition activity, particularly if freight market conditions weaken, thereby contributing to a gradual rebalancing of supply, the company said.

Prompt tonnage pressures Atlantic Basin Suezmax, Aframax rates
Aframax rates plunge w215
Largest single-day drop since at least March 2022
USGC-UKC Suezmax rate drops w180 to w295
Long-haul Aframax rates out of the US Gulf Coast dropped w215 Nov. 5 as available prompt tonnage finally relieved charterers of record-high rates, multiple sources said.
"Just watching the bottom fall out of this market," one shipbroker said. "Absolute bloodbath."
Fresh testing revealed how much ground charterers had gained back on the USGC-UK Continent run, with Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reporting fixtures at w425 and w325 during the morning of Nov. 5. Platts assessed the benchmark run at w325 Nov. 5, with w215 marking the largest single-day drop in the assessed value since at least March 10, 2022. The benchmark is at its lowest assessed value since Sept. 9 and is down w375 from Oct. 30.
"I see that trend continuing," a second shipbroker said. Growing prompt tonnage availability has given charterers more flexibility, multiple market participants noted to Platts this week. "Just a huge oversupply of tonnage," the first shipbroker said. "Owners were holding out for a while but I guess they caved." Charterers, meanwhile, were "holding out on cargoes," one shipowner told Platts Nov. 4. Two transatlantic cargoes were reported outstanding after the Nov. 5 Platts Market on Close assessment process. A third shipbroker described inquiry as "increased." A fourth shipbroker suggested Nov. 5 that the rate move was more related to the "big drop in oil prices." "Those weigh heavily to drop paper. Then you see charterers who trade paper heavily dump tonnage and rates," the fourth shipbroker said.
Suezmax rates drop Alongside the softened Aframax rates, freight also saw a
significant downward correction for the Suezmax segment amid bearish market fundamentals.
One shipbroker said that "a lengthy list of prompt vessels across both classes has strengthened charterers' negotiating position."
That availability of tonnage, coupled with limited amounts of inquiry, was initially seen with a reported 14 offers collected for a Suezmax Brazil-UKC voyage booked by Petrobras on Nov. 4, leading to a large downward correction on the run.
On Nov. 5, the steepest decline was seen on the Suezmax USGC-UKC run, which had not seen fresh testing in multiple weeks, with charterers instead preferring the Aframax option.
Vitol booked the Prudent Warrior for a Nov. 27-30 loading USGC-transatlantic run at w295, a route which saw indications largely in the w470-480 range just a day prior.

West of Suez clean tankers soften on tonnage, low inquiries
Med Handysize rates drop to $37.73/mt
Long position lists, thin cargo inquiries weigh
UK/Continent market weakens
The West of Suez clean tanker market was very quiet at the start of November, with market participants holding back amid uncertainty over the Middle East conflict and the US midterm elections, pushing freight rates lower across most routes, sources said.
Long position lists, scarce cargo inquiries and a reluctance among owners to fix characterized the market, leading to falling Handysize rates in both the Mediterranean and the UK/Continent regions, while Medium Range tankers in the Med remained firm.
Cross-Mediterranean Handysize rates fell to $37.73/metric ton on Nov. 4, down from $40.46/mt on Oct. 29, with market participants saying the East-West divergence in the Med Handysize market persisted amid broadly subdued activity. Some fixtures were still expected to emerge, but the paper market was already pointing lower, sources said, supporting the bearish sentiment.
"[It's] very quiet for now. Let's see what happens with the situation in the Middle East," a Dubai-based shipbroker said. "I reckon lots of players [are] sitting and waiting to see what happens after the US elections," a Dubai-based shipbroker said.
Some deals were being done directly between parties and hence were not visible in the market, market participants said. Owners also tend to go quiet when the market is softening, a London-based broker said, which can make it harder to gauge the true level of activity.
Another London-based shipbroker said the current quiet was not seasonal. They expected the market to pick up toward the end of November and into December with the pre-Christmas rush in refined product demand, which is the typical pattern.
Medium Range tankers in the Mediterranean were firmer. The Med-West Africa MR rate was assessed at $67.91/mt on Nov. 4, up from $62.10/mt Oct. 29, a rise of nearly 10% week over week.
In the UKC, the market was weaker, with the Cross-UKC Handysize route assessed at $28.98/mt on Nov. 4, down from $35.18/mt on Oct. 29 and the UKC-US Atlantic Coast MR trip down to $28.98/mt from $34.79/mt over the same period. Shipbrokers pointed to an absence of reported cargoes and a well-supplied tonnage list as the main reasons for the decline.

On the softening UKC rates, a third London-based shipbroker said last week's volume output did not match expectations, while the UKC-USAC forward freight agreement market had since fallen over two days. Tonnage had built up in the region, and while a fair few positions had been taken off the open position list, which might ease the tonnage list, it did not help sentiment when those cargoes were not being reported, the broker said. More volume was now being seen, but this was mainly because rates had fallen and charterers were catching the lower freight, they added.
Handysize rates compounded the pressure, having dropped sharply and reduced cross-continent demand for MRs, ultimately pulling the rest of the rate complex lower, the broker said. With the UKC-USAC arbitrage shut, less tonnage was being removed from the list and more was repopulating, they said.
"The tonnage list is well-supplied over the coming days, leaving little support for current levels unless fresh demand emerges," a Europe-based source said. The tonnage list was longer, and demand was slow in the UKC, adding that overall sentiment remained soft. A second Europe-based shipbroker said the naphtha arbitrage to the East was not working.
A shipowner said the market was "soft but should not drop too much." A third Europe-based shipbroker said: "Northwest Europe Handysizes are steady for now, already corrected quite a bit, and MRs are coming off."
-- Alara Tizer
Hormuz transits increase to 21 on Nov. 4 despite persistent ship attacks: CAS
12 vessels cross with disrupted AIS signals
Three ships attacked on Nov. 4, tugboat caught fire
Bab al-Mandab transits reach 36, four-day high
Hormuz vessel traffic rose to 21 crossings on Nov. 4 compared to 20 transits the day prior despite three tanker attacks occurring in the Gulf, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said Nov. 5.
Traffic through the Gulf Strait featured three crude tankers, one crude/products carrier, four product tankers, one LPG carrier, six bulk carriers, one container ship, three general cargo ships, one landing craft and one tug, according to CAS.
Most of the day's traffic moved outbound, with 15 ships exiting the Gulf while six entered, CAS data showed. Additionally, seven transiting vessels were linked to Iran, and 12 of the 21 that crossed during the day had limited or interrupted AIS signaling.
A total of three ships had been attacked in the Gulf and near the Strait of Hormuz over Nov. 4, according to reports, maintaining heightened risks for shipping in the region while negotiations to curb another flare-up of the war actively take place.
An oil tanker that attempted to cross the Strait of Hormuz using the southern route of the strait the evening of Nov. 4 was reportedly struck by a sea mine, caught fire and "stopped," Sepah News reported on Nov. 5, citing a statement by the IRGC naval forces from that day.

"Just like other tankers in recent days that hit naval mines and burst into flames, this vessel had switched off its identification and navigation systems," state-run Islamic Republic News Agency (IRNA) reported Nov. 5.

Crossings through the US naval blockade declined to 49 on Nov. 4 from 59 a day earlier, CAS reported. 26 vessels transited inbound while 23 moved outbound the blockade. Only one noncompliant vessel, a bulk carrier with a recent Iranian port call, was identified as having crossed during the day.

It exited the Gulf laden with Iranian LPG, CAS observed. It has appeared to operate as a shuttle vessel for Iranian LPG, "regularly moving cargoes out of the Gulf for onward transfer," CAS stated.
Banian is currently off Fujairah, according to CAS, which expects it to conduct a ship-to-ship transfer of its cargo near the UAE port.

Bab al-Mandab transits Ship activity at the Bab al-Mandab Strait also rebounded,
reaching 36 total transits compared with 32 on Nov. 3, CAS reported, marking the highest daily crossings tally in four days.
Traffic on Nov. 4 featured the Greek VLCC New Giant, which moved northbound into the Red Sea and is signaling for Suez, Egypt. CAS found that the tanker had been shipping Middle East crude to Asia, with a recent delivery of Iraqi crude to South Korea.
Red Sea Strait transits also included seven bulk carriers, five containerships and eight miscellaneous vessels, according to CAS.

Russia's state-run ship classifier wins partial court victory against EU sanctions
Court annuls EU recognition withdrawal ruling
Transaction, port entry bans remain in place
EU has 70 days to appeal court ruling
Russia's state-owned ship classification society has won a partial legal victory against EU sanctions that could make it easier for the organization to resume European activities if sanctions are eventually lifted.
Following Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, Brussels banned financial transactions with the Russian Maritime Register of Shipping and the entry of ships under its classification into EU ports, while stopping recognition of the validity of its inspections, surveys and certification, among other restrictive measures.
On Nov. 4, the EU Court of Justice annulled the bloc's withdrawal of recognition, ruling the measure was disproportionate because a temporary suspension could have achieved the same objective.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

32

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 5, 2026

The court said in a statement that following a full withdrawal, RMRS would have had to undergo a new recognition procedure if sanctions were lifted, rather than having its earlier recognition restored.
In a statement Nov. 5, RMRS confirmed the court ruling but added that the EU's provision will remain in effect for now as Brussels has two months and 10 days to appeal against the ruling.
In any case, the ruling would not restore normal European operations for RMRS. The court upheld the bulk of the EU's restrictive measures, including transaction bans. It also ruled inadmissible RMRS's challenge against the port entry ban.
St. Petersburg-based RMRS is Russia's largest ship classification society. Russian crude exports reached 116 million barrels on 142 tankers in October, and RMRS provided classification services to 77 of them, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea and Maritime Risk Intelligence Suite data.

Hormuz oil shipping unlikely to recover 100% even after peace achieved: NYK CEO
NYK expects Hormuz effectively closed till March 2027
Cape of Good Hope route continues as Suez bypass
ONE gradually increasing number of Red Sea transit: NYK CEO
Oil shipping via the Strait of Hormuz is unlikely to recover 100% even after a peace deal is achieved as oil companies step up their alternative oil procurement, President and CEO Takaya Soga of Japan's Nippon Yusen Kaisha, one of the world's largest ship operators, said Nov. 5.
"Even if peace is achieved with regard to Hormuz, I do not think things will simply return 100% to the way they were before. I believe that the diversification efforts currently underway will inevitably remain in place to some extent," Soga told an earnings press conference.
"With risks of this magnitude having actually materialized, oil companies and others are now working very hard to hedge those risks by developing alternatives and increasingly diversifying their sources of supply," Soga said.
When asked to comment on the situation in the Middle East following the US midterm elections, Soga said, "In reality, there are likely to be a host of issues that will not simply disappear even if peace is achieved."
"For example, there are believed to be a considerable number of mines in the Strait of Hormuz and there is also the question of whether the Iranian government will impose a surcharge on vessels transiting the strait. I think resolving these various issues will take a considerable amount of time," he said.
"Therefore, even if the situation moves toward peace, we do not believe that the conditions necessary to resume transit through the Strait of Hormuz will be put in place immediately. We therefore expect the situation in which vessels refrain from

transiting the strait to continue for some time." Soga's remarks came as NYK Line expects the Strait of
Hormuz will effectively be closed until the end of March 2027 as a basis for its income forecast for fiscal year 2026-27 (April-March), Chief Financial Officer Takuji Banno said Nov. 5.
"As for Hormuz, when we announced our previous forecast in August, we explained that it was based on the assumption that the situation would continue through the end of September and that passage through the Strait would become possible thereafter," Banno told the earnings press conference.
"This time, however, we have significantly revised that assumption and recast the forecast on the basis that passage through the Strait of Hormuz will remain impossible through the end of March this fiscal year," Banno added.
Suez transit NYK said it also expects the Cape of Good Hope route to
bypass the Suez Canal to continue through the end of March 2027, although some container ships have begun gradually resuming transit through the Suez Canal.
Japan's container conglomerate Ocean Network Express Pte. Ltd. (ONE), in which NYK has a 38% stake alongside 31% each held by Mitsui OSK Lines Ltd. and Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha, resumed a part of container shipping via the Suez Canal in October for the first time since its suspended navigation in December 2023, a NYK spokesperson said Nov. 5. The NYK spokesperson, however, added that the company has yet to let ONE charter out its owned ships for the Red Sea shipping.
As for the Suez Canal transit, Soga said, "It is true that the number is gradually increasing. But as far as container shipping is concerned, particularly on routes connecting Asia and Europe, I believe the current situation is that most container shipping operators are routing roughly half of their various service loops through the Red Sea, while using alternative routes for the remainder."
"ONE is also gradually increasing the number of service loops transiting the Red Sea because, from a competitive standpoint, it inevitably needs to resume such services. But I think that before long, even at ONE, the proportion of its service loops transiting the Red Sea will probably be around half," he said.
"Ultimately, rather than routing 100% of their vessels through the Red Sea and exposing themselves to the risk of losing everything in the event of an attack, operators are diversifying their risk. I think that is essentially the situation at present," Soga said, adding that NYK does not currently believe that the lives and safety of crew members can be adequately assured.
"Therefore, I think it will still be some time before we are in a position to decide to resume transits through here [the Red Sea]," he added.
-- Takeo Kumagai

The East of Suez Long Range tanker market extended its decline on Nov. 5 across both the LR1 and LR2 segments, as fresh cargoes continued to test lower freight levels.
In the LR2 segment, declining westbound rates added pressure to eastbound rates, with previously concluded levels viewed as repeatable, according to several market sources.
"There are ships in position that can do competitive rates, which could add further downward pressure, although some owners may find it difficult to accept such levels," an LR2 broker said.
Weakness in the dirty tanker market further weighed on sentiment across the Long Range 2 sector.
The LR1 market also softened as fresh fixtures tested lower levels.
The Hafnia Excel was placed on subjects by ADMIC as a replacement vessel for Nov. 11 loading from Sohar to Japan at w480.
Meanwhile, the same charterer took the Torm Eva on subjects for a 55,000 mt naphtha cargo loading Nov. 11 on the same route at w470, indicating further downward pressure on LR1 freight levels.

Southeast Asia MRs were mostly steady, with market participants citing limited fresh inquiry and ample tanker availability as factors weighing on sentiment. An MR broker said that several tankers could be ballasting from the region up to North Asia to load there instead.
North Asia MRs were assessed at lower levels, in line with market indications. Few cargoes were reported day over day, though in the near term, fresh inquiry could provide some support to rates, an MR broker said.
The Atlas T was placed on subjects at $1.95 million by Total for Nov. 19 loading on the South Korea-Singapore route, sources said. The ST Nikolai was placed on subjects at w650 by Ampol for Nov. 15 loading on the Japan-Australia route, sources added.
Gulf MRs were mostly assessed unchanged. With LRs seeing soft sentiment, MR freight levels are expected to be steady, market participants said. The Torm Allegro was placed on subjects at w525 by BP for Nov. 18 loading on the New MangaloreSingapore route, sources said.

Platts East of Suez Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
VLCC rates drop on weak long-haul demand
Suezmax freight falls
Aframax segment declines on quiet market activity
The East of Suez VLCC market fell further amid a slowdown in overall cargo inquiries Nov. 5, particularly for long-haul voyages, market sources said.

The Maran Athena was heard to have been placed on subjects by ST for Nov. 20 loading of 270,000 metric tons of crude from Fujairah to the Far East at w610, with Southeast Asia at w650.
Market sources expected rates to remain under pressure, with softer loading demand likely to drag levels further down.
"VLCC rates have continued to decline due to a reduction in long-haul voyages," a VLCC broker said.
"There is a lack of demand, with not many cargoes working across the market. I think this trend will continue in the short term, and we could see rates fall into the w500s," a second shipbroker said.
Several VLCC owners also pointed to growing tonnage supply as adding further pressure to the freight market.
Charterers were also heard weighing VLCC and Suezmax options, with rates for both vessel classes falling and competition between the two sizes adding further pressure, sources said.
Among fresh cargoes heard, BPCL was seeking a VLCC for Nov. 24 loading of 260,000 mt of crude from Yanbu to Sikka, laden via Bab el-Mandeb.
HPCL was also heard to have taken a VLCC for an inside Gulf loading, although further details were not immediately available.
The Suezmax market dropped sharply, pressured by weakness in the VLCC segment and a broader downward correction in global freight markets, sources said.
BPCL was heard seeking a Suezmax for Nov. 24 loading of 130,000 mt of crude from Yanbu to Sikka, laden via Bab el-Mandeb.
The Persian Gulf Aframax market also fell sharply, with significant downward corrections in the cross-Mediterranean market adding pressure to sentiment in the East, a shipbroker said.
Aframaxes slipped day over day amid quiet market activity and easing tonnage availability. PV Trans was reported seeking an Aframax for Nov. 23-25 loading from the Orkid-Kekwa range to Dungquat, according to market sources.
In a recent cargo, Shell was seeking an Aframax for Nov. 21-23 loading from Dampier to the Far East, though this could be covered by its own vessel instead, sources said. There was market chatter pointing to softening rates on West routes, but details were not available at the time of writing.
Medium Range was assessed unchanged day over day, in line with market indications.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Vickey Du, Ellie Lin

appeared to be slowly approaching a floor. Most November cargoes have now been covered, with
charterers looking toward December dates for eastbound voyages as the market has softened.
Fundamentals remained firmly in charterers' favor, with a lengthy tonnage list, soft adjacent markets offering little alternative employment, and negative sentiment stemming from off-market business.

Iran gives free pass to 4 countries to navigate Strait of Hormuz: MP
China, India, Qatar, Iraq given free passage
MP cites medicine, political support deals
Iran is allowing vessels from India, China, Iraq and Qatar to move through the Strait of Hormuz without restrictions, the deputy head of the parliament's national security and foreign policy committee, Amir Hayat-Moghaddam, said Nov. 3, according to the semi-official student news network SNN.

"These countries' ships freely transit and the Americans do not do anything to them either," Hayat-Moghaddam said.
"We are making deals here and each of these exemptions has a certain reason," he said, adding that India would export medicines to Iran and China would provide political support.
Meanwhile, Iran has increased attacks on vessels in and around the Strait of Hormuz in recent weeks as the conflict enters its ninth month, while the US continues to impose its own blockade line just beyond the strait that has choked off many of Iran's exports.
The Iranian Revolutionary Guard announced the destruction of three "violating oil tankers" in the Strait of Hormuz during the early hours of Nov. 3, while four other oil carriers were destroyed following a "mine collision" later that day, the IRGC's news carrier Sepah News reported on Nov. 4.
A Pakistan-flagged tanker owned by Qatar's national gas transport company Nakilat was struck Oct. 30 as it was sailing in the Gulf. Qatar's Foreign Ministry said at the time the attack was a "dangerous act" that "threatens the safety of maritime navigation and the security of global energy supplies."
Earlier, a vessel was struck 30 nautical miles off the coast of Iraq's port city Al Faw on Oct. 20, according to the UK Maritime Trade Operations.
On the same day, Iran's foreign minister Abbas Araghchi gave an interview to Iraqi state news in which he said Iran had opened "special routes" for Iraqi ships through the Strait of Hormuz to help Iraq mitigate the cost of the ongoing crisis in the waterway.
-- Lauren Holtmeier, Aresu Eqbali

JAPAN DATA: Refinery runs rise to 77% over Oct. 25-31 on higher crude throughput
Crude throughput rises 1% to 2.40 million b/d
Three refining units shut in Japan as of Oct. 31
Japan's refinery run rates rose to 77.1% over Oct. 25-31, from 76.4% the previous week, amid higher crude throughput, data from the Petroleum Association of Japan showed Nov. 5.

The country's crude throughput increased 0.9% week over week to 2.40 million barrels/day, the data showed.
As of Oct. 31, three crude distillation units were offline across Japan, according to calculations from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The capacity outage has remained unchanged since Oct. 7, when Japan's second-largest refiner, Idemitsu Kosan, restarted the sole CDU at its Keihin refinery in Tokyo Bay following scheduled maintenance.
Meanwhile, Idemitsu shut one of the CDUs at its 225,000 b/d Yokkaichi refinery in central Japan for planned maintenance on Sept. 28.
ENEOS, Japan's largest refiner, shut the 95,200 b/d No. 2 CDU at its 200,200 b/d Mizushima-B plant in western Japan Aug. 31, and the sole 153,000 b/d CDU at its Negishi refinery in Tokyo Bay Sept. 7, both for planned maintenance.
Japan's crude stocks rose 0.4% week over week but fell 3.4% year over year to 65.22 million barrels in the week to Oct. 31, PAJ and Platts data showed Nov. 5. Unfinished oil product stocks totaled 42.45 million barrels, up 0.2% week over week and 3.2% year over year, the data showed.
-- Hiroyuki Koshoji
Big Oil's integrated approach pays off as refining margins skyrocket
Iran war lifts refining margins, spurs volatility
Shell, BP cite importance of integrated approach
Majors eye capital discipline amid uncertainty
Western oil and gas majors were carried to yet another bumper quarter in Q3 by soaring refining margins -- even as upstream production continued to suffer from the Middle East conflict -- as their large integrated businesses, spanning the entire oil value chain, continued to pay dividends.
After the US-Israeli war on Iran began in late February, supermajors saw rocketing crude prices fill company coffers. As the war dragged on, denting Middle East production and raising freight costs, those with large trading teams were able to leverage the extreme volatility.
The third quarter was refining's turn. With product flows through the Strait of Hormuz at a minimum -- even as crude flows out started to pick up -- and stocks low across the board, diesel cracks repeatedly hit record highs. Majors in both Europe and the US put their refineries into overdrive.
Posting its earnings last week, BP said its refining margin hit $46.70/b in Q3, up almost threefold year over year, helping lift profits to multi-year highs despite flat upstream production and reduced throughput from its Gelsenkirchen divestment.
Shell saw a similar effect, with a record $42/b refining margin, while TotalEnergies' European margin more than doubled quarter over quarter to $33.20/b.
In the US, where companies have publicly resisted a potential diesel export ban amid high domestic prices, Chevron saw its

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

37

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 5, 2026

downstream profits more than triple year over year to almost $5 billion, it said, while ExxonMobil's refining margin soared to $50.30/b.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed European ultralow sulfur diesel on a CIF basis at $1,370.25/metric ton on Nov. 4, down from its mid-September peak of $1,642.25/mt.
The trend shows how the impact of the war in Iran is making its way through the oil value chain. Rather than a straightforward oil price windfall, the conflict is creating challenges and opportunities across upstream, refining, storage, shipping and trading, allowing large, integrated companies with exposure on all these fronts to capitalize.
Shell CEO Wael Sawan told analysts that the "integrated strength of Shell" had underpinned its recent performance, while BP's Meg O'Neill said her company's integrated model was its biggest asset in the current macro environment, "categorically denying" any discussions about a breakup of the UK major.
"The increase of nearly 10% refining throughput enabled the company to fully capture historically high refining margins in Europe and worldwide, while oil trading activities outperformed at a level comparable with the two previous quarters," TotalEnergies said.
However, the majors did not commit to expanding their downstream footprints, even though limited investment and recent closures have limited spare refining capacity.
Rather, TotalEnergies' Patrick Pouyanne said the crisis had encouraged the French company "to maybe consider maintaining these capacities."
Shell CFO Sinead Gorman cautioned that high freight costs are already eating into margins, with some expecting softer product cracks in Q4, while upstream production remains challenged by Middle East tensions. ExxonMobil said the conflict reduced its Q3 output by 350,000 b/d of oil equivalent, while some of the 100,000 b/d BP lost -- mostly from Iraqi fields -- started to ease in the third quarter, O'Neill said.
Capital discipline In exploration and production, the quarter saw differing
approaches among the European and American majors, with ExxonMobil and Chevron flagging a focus on maximizing Permian

output, while their European rivals scour the globe for deepwater, LNG, and brownfield projects.
ExxonMobil hopes to end 2026 with 1.8 million boe/d of production in Texas' Permian Basin and raise it to over 2.5 million boe/d by 2030, it said, with a focus on automating drilling rigs and improving shale recovery rates.
For its part, Chevron has held Permian production near 1 million boe/d for six consecutive quarters and hopes to extend the plateau through improved reservoir management, while growing global output by 2-3% by 2030.
In Europe, Italy's Eni lifted its 2026 production growth target to 6-7% and sees 2026 project sanctions adding around 600,000 boe/d by 2030, with assets across Africa, Asia, South America and the East Mediterranean, it said.
TotalEnergies is eyeing projects from Nigeria to Namibia to meet its 3% annual growth target, and is focused on expanding LNG output, while BP is balancing infrastructure-led exploration with selective greenfield projects, including its 8-billionbarrel Bumerangue discovery off Brazil, which O'Neill said was technically complex.
Crucially, the next phase of upstream competition could see majors prioritize returns over production growth. Sawan told analysts that Shell was looking at how to "restock" its reserves through exploration with a "focus on value rather than volume", while O'Neill has said BP previously stretched itself too thin.
And despite the Iran war juicing Big Oil profits in the short term, majors remain focused on capital discipline, with most retaining their 2026 capex guidance and using strong profits to pay down debt and return cash to shareholders.
Price strength The quarter also reflected the recent shift in tone around
peak oil and energy security, with the European majors refocusing on core oil and gas, after years of increased investments in renewables.
Sawan said "energy security has taken priority in thinking" among host countries this year. "Countries are more and more looking to build out their energy infrastructure", realizing its importance for digital and industrial strategies and national security," he said.

November 5, 2026

Meanwhile, oil major CEOs expect markets to remain tight in the coming months due to extremely low stocks.
Sawan said Shell was seeing "more and more structural stability in higher oil prices," noting that depleted global inventories would need to be restocked and then increased "for energy security purposes."
Platts Dated Brent was last assessed at $96.16/b on Nov. 4, down from a recent peak of over $135/b in early October, as the US Midterm elections weighed on prices.

BP trims stake in Norway's Aker BP amid portfolio rethink
Sells 5% Aker BP stake for $1.06 billion
Iran war fuels demand for Norwegian oil
CEO O'Neill refocuses on core oil, gas
BP has sold off almost a third of its stake in Norwegian producer Aker BP, it said in a statement Nov. 5, as part of a portfolio simplification process under new CEO Meg O'Neill.
The British supermajor said it had sold a 5% stake in the company - one of Norway's largest oil and gas producers - out of its 15.9% shareholding, leaving it with an interest of approximately 10.9%.
The sale, at a price of NOK 311 per share, has raised NOK 9.83 billion, BP said, or $1.06 billion. The firm has entered into a 90-day lock-up for its remaining shares, it said.
The company, which is undergoing a strategic rethink under O'Neill - who took over in April to right the ship after a largely unsuccessful shift into clean energy - said the proceeds will go towards strengthening its balance sheet.
The company is therefore set to divest around $9 billion in 2026, some $1 billion higher than its previous guidance.
"Since Aker BP's creation a decade ago, it has executed a successful growth strategy that has led to significant value creation," O'Neill said in the statement.
"In recent months, it has further increased its value through a combination of its strong performance and the favorable macro environment. We believe now is the right time for us to realize part of this value to further strengthen BP's balance sheet, our first priority as we build a simpler, stronger, more valuable BP."
The US-Israeli war on Iran has sent crude and refined product prices soaring this year, with Middle Eastern facilities forced to close and commodity flows through the critical Strait of Hormuz severely hampered.
As a result, Norwegian producers such as Aker BP have been able to sell cargoes into Europe at hefty premiums to Dated Brent, which Platts last assessed at $96.16/b on Nov. 4, down from a recent peak of over $135/b in early October. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Aker BP, whose portfolio includes a slew of Norwegian fields and a stake in the 755,000 barrel/day Johan Sverdrup field said it achieved a liquids price in Q3 of $102/b, up from $70/b a year earlier.

The company produced 374,000 b/d of oil equivalent in the quarter, it said last month. It also recently agreed to join forces with rivals Equinor and Var Energi to explore for frontier standalone oil and gas developments on the Norwegian Continental Shelf.
The Aker BP share sale comes just days after BP reported soaring profits in the third quarter and tapped Shell's chemicals head to run its downstream division.
O'Neill has simplified BP's structure into two core divisions, upstream and downstream, and signaled the company would refocus on oil and gas.
She has laid out plans to make the company simpler, stronger and more valuable, with decisions based on data and value, not historic ties, as exemplified by an ongoing process to sell its UK North Sea oil and gas business.
-- Charlie Mitchell

REFINERY NEWS: Sunoco LP reports strong Burnaby margins as US fuel demand holds
Refinery: Burnaby, British Columbia
Owner: Sunoco LP
Overall capacity: 58,000 b/d
Units affected: N/A
Duration: Q3 2026
US fuel demand has stayed "remarkably resilient" despite heavy price volatility, while Canadian demand is seen as softer, a Sunoco LP executive said Nov. 3.
During Sunoco's third-quarter results call, Chief Commercial Officer Austin Harkness cited monthly data from the US Energy Information Administration showing diesel demand about flat year over year and gasoline demand down in the low single digits, with margins still healthy, but noted that price volatility has been "amplified in Canada relative to what we've seen in the US."

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

39

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 5, 2026

"As an example, diesel recently touched $7/gallon, USDequivalent there. And so some of the themes that we had touched on last quarter around demand starting to soften continue into Q3. So diesel and gasoline demand is off by low to mid-single digits. But as a reminder, it's a very strong margin environment," he said.
At the Burnaby Refinery, located in British Columbia, Canada, throughput averaged 57,000 barrels/day, or 99% crude utilization. Crude throughput was 54,000 b/d while biofuel feedstock throughput was 3,000 b/d. Refining margins for the quarter topped $54/b, and expenses were below $10/b for a second straight quarter.
Sunoco acquired the refinery in 2025 as part of the $9.1 billion Parkland transaction, along with Parkland's Canadian transportation infrastructure and Caribbean fuel distribution business.
Sunoco's pipeline and terminal segments were steady, the company said.
Sunoco's Q3 pipeline throughput averaged about 1.333 million b/d in Q3, slightly below the 1.347 million b/d in Q2, but above 1.296 million b/d in Q3 2025, reflecting the "impact of modest growth across most pipeline systems."
Sunoco's Q3 terminal throughput averaged 1 million b/d in Q3, about flat with Q2.
Sunoco's fuel distribution segment sold 4.2 billion gallons of fuel in Q3, with motor fuel profit of 16.8 cent/gal, which is down slightly from 17.1 cents/gal profit in Q2. This compares with the 2.295 billion gallons of fuel sold in Q3 2025 at a profit of 10.7 cents/gal due to the acquisition of Parkland.
Sunoco LP was formed in 2014 after Sunoco was acquired in 2012 by midstream operator Energy Transfer, which then combined Sunoco's assets with those of Susser Petroleum Partners LP to create one of North America's largest fuel distributors and energy infrastructure operators. The 2025 acquisition of Parkland and 2024 acquisition of NuStar boosted its pipeline and terminal capacity.
Sunoco closed the $600 million acquisition of Offen Petroleum last week, adding 2.5 billion gallons/year of fuel distribution volume and strengthening its presence in the Midwest and Mountain West.
"Offen gives us scale in areas of the US that we haven't necessarily had scale before," said COO Karl Fails.
"So that really leads into the next benefit, which is supply optionality. So that larger scale in the Midwest and Mountain West region really integrates well with our footprint in Canada, the South, the Southwest, and a little bit farther east. So it provides a lot of options for us to supply and integrate those markets into our much broader network," Fails added.
Fails said Sunoco could see some benefits from the planned pipelines serving the US West Coast -- such as the joint-venture Western Gateway -- as well as the Denver products pipeline expansion. He said it could tighten Mid-Continent balances and lift northbound Gulf Coast shipments.
-- Janet Mcgurty

REFINERY NEWS: Calumet revises Montana Renewables expansion through asset lease
Refinery: Great Falls, Montana
Owner: Calumet
Overall capacity: 12,000 b/d petroleum; 7,000 b/d renewables
Units affected: N/A
Duration: 2026-2028
Calumet Inc. has revised the ownership and financing structure of its Montana Renewables business as it moves ahead with a lower-cost expansion to boost sustainable aviation fuel production to about 200 million gallons/year by the end of 2028, the company said in a Nov. 5 statement.
"These transactions strengthen our ability to advance Montana Renewables by providing the critical assets essential to the MaxSAF expansion and the capture of 70% of the originally expected economic project benefit for approximately 15% of the planned capital," said CEO Todd Borgmann in the statement.
"We appreciate the U.S. Department of Energy and Warburg Pincus, whose continued partnership and support provide additional flexibility as we execute our revised expansion plan. Together, we are strengthening Montana Renewables' position as one of the world's largest SAF producers while supporting American agriculture, the Montana business community, our employees, and our shareholders," Borgmann said.
In September, Calumet said it had revised its project to cut the cost by 85%, and Montana Renewables renegotiated its US Department of Energy loan as SAF project capital costs fell.
Under the Nov. 5 amended agreement, Warburg Pincus LLC will receive richer preferred-equity economics in exchange for relinquishing its current common-equity position. Its preferred units will increase to about $395 million from roughly $346 million, while the preferred return rises to 11.5% from 8%.
The changes come alongside a new long-term asset lease between Calumet Montana Refining LLC, a restricted subsidiary of Calumet, and Montana Renewables LLC. The renewable fuels business will lease existing refinery equipment, including a hydrotreater, hydrogen plant and naphtha splitter, from the adjacent conventional refining operation. The lease is expected to provide Calumet Montana Refining with about $30 million/year of income, subject to adjustments.
Repurposing the existing equipment is a central part of Calumet's revised MaxSAF expansion strategy, substantially reducing the capital required for the project.
The company said remaining project spending is expected to total $137 million, compared with the $1.2 billion megaproject contemplated under the original loan issued by the DOE.
The company expects the revised project to capture about 70% of the economic benefit envisioned under the original expansion while requiring approximately 15% of the planned capital.
The expansion is ultimately designed to raise Montana Renewables' SAF production to about 200 million gal/year and total product sales to 17,000 b/d by year-end 2028.

Suncor has begun planned work at its 103,000 b/d Commerce City refinery near Denver, the only refinery in Colorado, the company has reported.
"Suncor Commerce City will be conducting maintenance over the next week," Suncor said in a Nov. 4 community notice. "This may result in increased flaring."
No further information was provided and a company spokesperson was not immediately available for comment. In October, Suncor also performed unspecified planned work the plant.
However, following some environmental issues at the plant, Suncor has been investing in upgrading the plant.
On Suncor's Nov. 4 third-quarter results call, CFO Peter Zebedee noted that the Commerce City team in July identified and addressed the constraints of furnaces at Plant 3, which raised by 5,000 b/d throughput to 43,000 b/d.
A similar project is underway at another plant at the refinery, Suncor's head of refining, David Oldreive, said on the call.
"We're doing similar things in Plant 2 with some new crudes that help fill up the bottom of our crude pipe fill," Oldreive said.
According to its website, Suncor has several projects planned for the refinery, including the routing of the main flare, which began in 2025 and is expected to be completed in 2027.
-- Janet Mcgurty

Overall capacity: 3.3 million mt/year (66,000 b/d) Poland's largest refiner Orlen SA said Nov. 5 its third-quarter
throughput fell 1% year over year to 10.083 million mt and utilization of its refineries was stable at 94%, as higher crude processing in Lithuania and the Czech Republic partially offset lower Polish throughput due to planned maintenance there.
Orlen CFO Slawomir Jedrzejczyk told a Q3 results conference the company carried out maintenance on the hydrocracking and gudron desulfurization unit at Plock in the third quarter and maintenance also reduced upstream output. The volatile geopolitical environment made for a challenging quarter, he said.
"It was yet another dynamic quarter. The fourth quarter started in an interesting way as well, but we are well prepared operationally and financially," Jedrzejczyk said. "We are pretty optimistic and we want to prove we can navigate this challenging volatile environment."
Refining fuel sales to external customers fell 5% year over year to 7.992 million metric tons.
Throughput at the Plock and Gdansk refineries fell 7% year over year to 5.6 million mt due to maintenance at Plock and utilization decreased by 6 percentage points to 94%. Middle and light distillates yield was up 5 percentage points to 87%.
Throughput at Orlen's Czech Unipetrol refineries rose 14% year over year to 1.88 million mt. Utilization of the refineries grew 11 percentage points to 86% and middle and light distillates yield was up 2 percentage points to 85%.
Throughput at Lithuania's Orlen Lietuva refinery rose 4% to 2.55 million mt. Utilization was up 4 percentage points to 98% and middle and light distillate yield was flat at 80%.
The macroeconomic environment saw continued improvement in Q3 as geopolitical instability raised operating costs and crude oil prices, improving refining margins and cracks, Orlen said. The higher crack spreads were partially offset by higher Arab Light crude oil prices, the 3% appreciation of the Polish zloty against the US dollar and hedging transactions. Orlen said its CEE refining margin indicator rose 172% year over year to $35.90/ barrel on a 40% rise in Brent crude oil prices.
The company said gasoline consumption in Orlen's 7 markets was flat year over year at 7.9 million mt, while diesel demand fell 5% to 17.4 million mt. Orlen's retail fuel sales fell 2% year over year to 2.94 million mt, with lower margins in Poland offset by higher margins on other markets.
